I’m not sure… I mean, overwhelming urge sounds a little stronger than what I’d say. I do sometimes feel like I need to check things, but that’s also my job, so it gets blurry.

I guess I don’t have a ritual exactly. Not like I have to tap something or count or do a set thing. I mostly just get anxious and try to look around and make sure I know what’s actually there.

I work as a building inspector, so I’m used to looking for what’s unsafe or what might fail, and lately it feels like my brain is doing that constantly, even when I’m not working.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s not even like I’m sitting there trying to think about it. I’ll be doing something normal at work, like measuring a railing, or I’ll hear a ladder scrape somewhere, and suddenly I can see my husband falling like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nAnd then my stomach just drops and my hands get shaky. It’s embarrassing because I’m supposed to be the calm one on a site, you know, checking things, making sure everything’s up to code… but my mind just goes there before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to make the thoughts or images of your husband falling go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way exactly… like I don’t have a specific thing I do every time to make it stop. I mostly just try to get back to whatever I was doing, or I’ll look around the site and remind myself what’s actually in front of me.\n\nIt’s funny, inspectors are always making little checklists—well, not funny, but you know what I mean—and sometimes I catch myself wanting to check everything around me, but I’m not sure if that’s about making the image go away or just… habit from work. So I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, overwhelming urge sounds a little stronger than what I’d say. I do sometimes feel like I need to check things, but that’s also my job, so it gets blurry.\n\nLike on a site, I’m always looking at guardrails and stairs and harness points anyway, and then I’ll remember something from this one renovation where the contractor swore everything was “fine,” and it absolutely wasn’t—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t have a ritual exactly. Not like I have to tap something or count or do a set thing. I mostly just get anxious and try to look around and make sure I know what’s actually there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. about the repair or look at the spot where it happened.\n- key: S072\n  name: Negative_Beliefs\n  description: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.\n  manifestation: I keep thinking that if I missed something at my own house, then maybe I'm not as careful or competent as I thought I was, even when my work reports don't show that.\n- key: S074\n  name: Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State\n  description: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).\n  manifestation: What brings me in is that I can't shake this heavy mix of fear and guilt, like I'm tense all day waiting for something else to go wrong and blaming myself for not stopping what happened.\n- key: S077\n  name: Irritability_Aggression\n contractor brushes off a safety issue or my husband asks the same thing again, I snap harder than I mean to and feel awful afterward.\n- key: S078\n  name: Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior\n thoughts or images of your husband falling go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way exactly… like I don’t have a specific thing I do every time to make it stop. I mostly just try to get back to whatever I was doing, or I’ll look around the site and remind myself what’s actually in front of me.\n\nIt’s funny, inspectors are always making little checklists—well, not funny, but you know what I mean—and sometimes I catch myself wanting to check everything around me, but I’m not sure if that’s about making the image go away or just… habit from work. So I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, overwhelming urge sounds a little stronger than what I’d say. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure. I mean, in my job, checking things is literally how you prevent something bad from happening, so it’s hard for me to separate that out.

Like if I’m looking at a railing twice, is that because I’m worried someone’s going to fall, or because the railing actually needs to be checked? I don’t always know. Sometimes I think I’m just doing what I’m supposed to do, and then my mind kind of… goes somewhere else.

So, maybe a little? But I wouldn’t say I have a clear thought like, “If I don’t check this, something terrible will happen.” It’s more muddled than that.

Like I’ll be measuring a railing or hear a ladder scrape, and suddenly I can see him falling like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there choosing to replay it. I might be doing something completely normal, and then my stomach drops and my hands get shaky, and I’m just… there with it. Then I have to try to pull myself back to what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking things around you is a way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, in my job, checking things is literally how you prevent something bad from happening, so it’s hard for me to separate that out.\n\nLike if I’m looking at a railing twice, is that because I’m worried someone’s going to fall, or because the railing actually needs to be checked? I don’t always know. Sometimes I think I’m just doing what I’m supposed to do, and then my mind kind of… goes somewhere else.\n\nSo, maybe a little? But I wouldn’t say I have a clear thought like, “If I don’t check this, something terrible will happen.” It’s more muddled than that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like something terrible will happen if you don't check things around you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 31 / construction_or_building_inspector / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I’d put it that strongly. I don’t usually think, like, “If I don’t check this exact thing, something terrible is going to happen.”

It’s more that if something seems off, I get this uncomfortable feeling and I want to look again. But then, in construction, that’s also just… normal? You’re supposed to notice things before they become a problem. I had a supervisor once who used to say, “If you walk past it, you own it,” and that kind of sticks in your head.

So… I’m not sure. Sometimes checking calms me down, but I don’t know if I believe it’s preventing some disaster right then.

It feels more like it’s being pushed on me, I guess. Not like a thought I’m choosing, or something that feels like “me” exactly.

I work as a building inspector, so I’m used to looking for what’s unsafe or what might fail, and lately it feels like my brain is doing that constantly, even when I’m not working.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s not even like I’m sitting there trying to think about it. I’ll be doing something normal at work, like measuring a railing, or I’ll hear a ladder scrape somewhere, and suddenly I can see my husband falling like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nAnd then my stomach just drops and my hands get shaky. It’s embarrassing because I’m supposed to be the calm one on a site, you know, checking things, making sure everything’s up to code… but my mind just goes there before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to make the thoughts or images of your husband falling go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way exactly… like I don’t have a specific thing I do every time to make it stop. I mostly just try to get back to whatever I was doing, or I’ll look around the site and remind myself what’s actually in front of me.\n\nIt’s funny, inspectors are always making little checklists—well, not funny, but you know what I mean—and sometimes I catch myself wanting to check everything around me, but I’m not sure if that’s about making the image go away or just… habit from work. So I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, overwhelming urge sounds a little stronger than what I’d say. I do sometimes feel like I need to check things, but that’s also my job, so it gets blurry.\n\nLike on a site, I’m always looking at guardrails and stairs and harness points anyway, and then I’ll remember something from this one renovation where the contractor swore everything was “fine,” and it absolutely wasn’t—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t have a ritual exactly. Not like I have to tap something or count or do a set thing. I mostly just get anxious and try to look around and make sure I know what’s actually there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are something that you want to think about?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t want to think about it. It just kind of barges in. Like I’ll be measuring a railing or hear a ladder scrape, and suddenly I can see him falling like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there choosing to replay it. I might be doing something completely normal, and then my stomach drops and my hands get shaky, and I’m just… there with it. Then I have to try to pull myself back to what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking things around you is a way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, in my job, checking things is literally how you prevent something bad from happening, so it’s hard for me to separate that out.\n\nLike if I’m looking at a railing twice, is that because I’m worried someone’s going to fall, or because the railing actually needs to be checked? I don’t always know. Sometimes I think I’m just doing what I’m supposed to do, and then my mind kind of… goes somewhere else.\n\nSo, maybe a little? But I wouldn’t say I have a clear thought like, “If I don’t check this, something terrible will happen.” It’s more muddled than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something terrible will happen if you don't check things around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that strongly. I don’t usually think, like, “If I don’t check this exact thing, something terrible is going to happen.”\n\nIt’s more that if something seems off, I get this uncomfortable feeling and I want to look again. But then, in construction, that’s also just… normal? You’re supposed to notice things before they become a problem. I had a supervisor once who used to say, “If you walk past it, you own it,” and that kind of sticks in your head.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Sometimes checking calms me down, but I don’t know if I believe it’s preventing some disaster right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are a part of you or something that is being imposed on you?\n\nPatient: It feels more like it’s being pushed on me, I guess. Not like a thought I’m choosing, or something that feels like “me” exactly.\n\nLike I’ll be measuring a railing, or even just hear a ladder scrape nearby, and then out of nowhere I can see my husband falling, like it’s happening right in front of me. And my stomach just drops, my hands get shaky… it’s almost embarrassing because I can be standing there with a tape measure trying to look professional, and suddenly I’m not really thinking about the job anymore.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t feel like a normal part of my thinking. It feels like it cuts in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll look around or kind of re-check what’s near me, but I don’t know if that’s me responding to the thought or just doing the job.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… falling is something I think about sometimes because of my work. Railings, ladders, stairs — that’s normal on a site. You kind of have to think about it, or somebody gets hurt. I’ve seen inspectors miss stuff and then everyone acts like it was obvious after the fact, which is… sorry, that’s not really the point.

The thoughts about my husband, though, don’t feel like my normal “check the railing” kind of thoughts. I’ll be measuring something, or even just hear a ladder scrape, and all of a sudden I can see him falling, like it’s happening right in front of me. My stomach drops and my hands get shaky.

So it’s not completely foreign in the sense that falling is part of my world, but the way it hits me — that feels foreign. It feels like it comes out of nowhere.

Sometimes checking calms me down, but I don’t know if I believe it’s preventing some disaster right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are a part of you or something that is being imposed on you?\n\nPatient: It feels more like it’s being pushed on me, I guess. Not like a thought I’m choosing, or something that feels like “me” exactly.\n\nLike I’ll be measuring a railing, or even just hear a ladder scrape nearby, and then out of nowhere I can see my husband falling, like it’s happening right in front of me. And my stomach just drops, my hands get shaky… it’s almost embarrassing because I can be standing there with a tape measure trying to look professional, and suddenly I’m not really thinking about the job anymore.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t feel like a normal part of my thinking. It feels like it cuts in.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to do something in response to these thoughts of your husband falling to make yourself feel better?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll look around or kind of re-check what’s near me, but I don’t know if that’s me responding to the thought or just doing the job.\n\nLike the other day I was on a site and there was this temporary stair setup, and I started thinking about how my husband always skips steps when he’s in a hurry—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess I don’t have some set thing I do to make the thought go away.\n\nI might pause, take a breath, maybe look at whatever triggered it again. But I don’t know if I’d call it “having to” do something. It’s more like I get rattled and then try to get back to what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are something that you would normally think about, or are they completely foreign to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… falling is something I think about sometimes because of my work. Railings, ladders, stairs — that’s normal on a site. You kind of have to think about it, or somebody gets hurt. I’ve seen inspectors miss stuff and then everyone acts like it was obvious after the fact, which is… sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe thoughts about my husband, though, don’t feel like my normal “check the railing” kind of thoughts. I’ll be measuring something, or even just hear a ladder scrape, and all of a sudden I can see him falling, like it’s happening right in front of me. My stomach drops and my hands get shaky.\n\nSo it’s not completely foreign in the sense that falling is part of my world, but the way it hits me — that feels foreign. It feels like it comes out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are forcing their way into your mind without you wanting them to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a good way to put it. Yes, unwanted is exactly it. I don’t mean to think about it.

I’ll be doing something normal, like measuring a railing, or I’ll just hear a ladder scrape nearby — which happens all the time on sites, people are always dragging stuff around — and then out of nowhere I can see my husband falling. Then I have to try to pull myself back to what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking things around you is a way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, in my job, checking things is literally how you prevent something bad from happening, so it’s hard for me to separate that out.\n\nLike if I’m looking at a railing twice, is that because I’m worried someone’s going to fall, or because the railing actually needs to be checked? I don’t always know. Sometimes I think I’m just doing what I’m supposed to do, and then my mind kind of… goes somewhere else.\n\nSo, maybe a little? But I wouldn’t say I have a clear thought like, “If I don’t check this, something terrible will happen.” It’s more muddled than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something terrible will happen if you don't check things around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that strongly. I don’t usually think, like, “If I don’t check this exact thing, something terrible is going to happen.”\n\nIt’s more that if something seems off, I get this uncomfortable feeling and I want to look again. But then, in construction, that’s also just… normal? You’re supposed to notice things before they become a problem. I had a supervisor once who used to say, “If you walk past it, you own it,” and that kind of sticks in your head.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Sometimes checking calms me down, but I don’t know if I believe it’s preventing some disaster right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are a part of you or something that is being imposed on you?\n\nPatient: It feels more like it’s being pushed on me, I guess. Not like a thought I’m choosing, or something that feels like “me” exactly.\n\nLike I’ll be measuring a railing, or even just hear a ladder scrape nearby, and then out of nowhere I can see my husband falling, like it’s happening right in front of me. And my stomach just drops, my hands get shaky… it’s almost embarrassing because I can be standing there with a tape measure trying to look professional, and suddenly I’m not really thinking about the job anymore.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t feel like a normal part of my thinking. It feels like it cuts in.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to do something in response to these thoughts of your husband falling to make yourself feel better?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll look around or kind of re-check what’s near me, but I don’t know if that’s me responding to the thought or just doing the job.\n\nLike the other day I was on a site and there was this temporary stair setup, and I started thinking about how my husband always skips steps when he’s in a hurry—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess I don’t have some set thing I do to make the thought go away.\n\nI might pause, take a breath, maybe look at whatever triggered it again. But I don’t know if I’d call it “having to” do something. It’s more like I get rattled and then try to get back to what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are something that you would normally think about, or are they completely foreign to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… falling is something I think about sometimes because of my work. Railings, ladders, stairs — that’s normal on a site. You kind of have to think about it, or somebody gets hurt. I’ve seen inspectors miss stuff and then everyone acts like it was obvious after the fact, which is… sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe thoughts about my husband, though, don’t feel like my normal “check the railing” kind of thoughts. I’ll be measuring something, or even just hear a ladder scrape, and all of a sudden I can see him falling, like it’s happening right in front of me. My stomach drops and my hands get shaky.\n\nSo it’s not completely foreign in the sense that falling is part of my world, but the way it hits me — that feels foreign. It feels like it comes out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are forcing their way into your mind without you wanting them to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a good way to put it. It’s not like I sit there deciding to imagine it.\n\nI’ll just be doing something normal, like measuring a railing, or I’ll hear a ladder scrape somewhere nearby, and then it’s just there — like I can see my husband falling right in front of me. No… I mean, I don’t want him hurt. That’s the opposite of what I believe or want, obviously. Sorry, that sounds too obvious.

I work as a building inspector, so I’m used to looking for what’s unsafe or what might fail, and lately it feels like my brain is doing that constantly, even when I’m not working.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s not even like I’m sitting there trying to think about it. I’ll be doing something normal at work, like measuring a railing, or I’ll hear a ladder scrape somewhere, and suddenly I can see my husband falling like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nAnd then my stomach just drops and my hands get shaky. It’s embarrassing because I’m supposed to be the calm one on a site, you know, checking things, making sure everything’s up to code… but my mind just goes there before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to make the thoughts or images of your husband falling go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way exactly… like I don’t have a specific thing I do every time to make it stop. I mostly just try to get back to whatever I was doing, or I’ll look around the site and remind myself what’s actually in front of me.\n\nIt’s funny, inspectors are always making little checklists—well, not funny, but you know what I mean—and sometimes I catch myself wanting to check everything around me, but I’m not sure if that’s about making the image go away or just… habit from work. So I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, overwhelming urge sounds a little stronger than what I’d say. I do sometimes feel like I need to check things, but that’s also my job, so it gets blurry.\n\nLike on a site, I’m always looking at guardrails and stairs and harness points anyway, and then I’ll remember something from this one renovation where the contractor swore everything was “fine,” and it absolutely wasn’t—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t have a ritual exactly. Not like I have to tap something or count or do a set thing. I mostly just get anxious and try to look around and make sure I know what’s actually there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are something that you want to think about?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t want to think about it. It just kind of barges in. Like I’ll be measuring a railing or hear a ladder scrape, and suddenly I can see him falling like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there choosing to replay it. I might be doing something completely normal, and then my stomach drops and my hands get shaky, and I’m just… there with it. Then I have to try to pull myself back to what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking things around you is a way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, in my job, checking things is literally how you prevent something bad from happening, so it’s hard for me to separate that out.\n\nLike if I’m looking at a railing twice, is that because I’m worried someone’s going to fall, or because the railing actually needs to be checked? I don’t always know. Sometimes I think I’m just doing what I’m supposed to do, and then my mind kind of… goes somewhere else.\n\nSo, maybe a little? But I wouldn’t say I have a clear thought like, “If I don’t check this, something terrible will happen.” It’s more muddled than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something terrible will happen if you don't check things around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that strongly. I don’t usually think, like, “If I don’t check this exact thing, something terrible is going to happen.”\n\nIt’s more that if something seems off, I get this uncomfortable feeling and I want to look again. But then, in construction, that’s also just… normal? You’re supposed to notice things before they become a problem. I had a supervisor once who used to say, “If you walk past it, you own it,” and that kind of sticks in your head.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Sometimes checking calms me down, but I don’t know if I believe it’s preventing some disaster right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are a part of you or something that is being imposed on you?\n\nPatient: It feels more like it’s being pushed on me, I guess. Not like a thought I’m choosing, or something that feels like “me” exactly.\n\nLike I’ll be measuring a railing, or even just hear a ladder scrape nearby, and then out of nowhere I can see my husband falling, like it’s happening right in front of me. And my stomach just drops, my hands get shaky… it’s almost embarrassing because I can be standing there with a tape measure trying to look professional, and suddenly I’m not really thinking about the job anymore.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t feel like a normal part of my thinking. It feels like it cuts in.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to do something in response to these thoughts of your husband falling to make yourself feel better?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll look around or kind of re-check what’s near me, but I don’t know if that’s me responding to the thought or just doing the job.\n\nLike the other day I was on a site and there was this temporary stair setup, and I started thinking about how my husband always skips steps when he’s in a hurry—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess I don’t have some set thing I do to make the thought go away.\n\nI might pause, take a breath, maybe look at whatever triggered it again. But I don’t know if I’d call it “having to” do something. It’s more like I get rattled and then try to get back to what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are something that you would normally think about, or are they completely foreign to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… falling is something I think about sometimes because of my work. Railings, ladders, stairs — that’s normal on a site. You kind of have to think about it, or somebody gets hurt. I’ve seen inspectors miss stuff and then everyone acts like it was obvious after the fact, which is… sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe thoughts about my husband, though, don’t feel like my normal “check the railing” kind of thoughts. I’ll be measuring something, or even just hear a ladder scrape, and all of a sudden I can see him falling, like it’s happening right in front of me. My stomach drops and my hands get shaky.\n\nSo it’s not completely foreign in the sense that falling is part of my world, but the way it hits me — that feels foreign. It feels like it comes out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are forcing their way into your mind without you wanting them to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a good way to put it. It’s not like I sit there deciding to imagine it.\n\nI’ll just be doing something normal, like measuring a railing, or I’ll hear a ladder scrape somewhere nearby, and then it’s just there — like I can see my husband falling right in front of me. And then my stomach drops and my hands get shaky.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’m supposed to be focused on the inspection, and instead my brain just kind of yanks me over to that image without asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are unwanted and are interrupting your other thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes, unwanted is exactly it. I don’t mean to think about it.\n\nI’ll be doing something normal, like measuring a railing, or I’ll just hear a ladder scrape nearby — which happens all the time on sites, people are always dragging stuff around — and then out of nowhere I can see my husband falling. Like it’s happening right in front of me. And then my stomach just drops and my hands get shaky.\n\nSo yeah, it interrupts whatever I was thinking about. I’ll be trying to focus on the measurement or what I’m supposed to write down, and suddenly I’m not really there for a second.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the thoughts about your husband falling are consistent with your own beliefs and values?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, I don’t want him hurt. That’s the opposite of what I believe or want, obviously. Sorry, that sounds too obvious.\n\nIt’s more like—I can be doing my job, measuring a railing, or I hear a ladder scrape, and then suddenly I see him falling like it’s happening right there. My stomach drops, my hands get shaky, and I’m not choosing it.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t feel like it matches me or what I value. It just barges in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think it’s part of who I am. It feels more like something that jumps in front of me.

Like I can be measuring a railing—actually, I was doing that last week and the tape kept slipping, which was annoying—and then I hear a ladder scrape somewhere, and suddenly I can see my husband falling like it’s happening right in front of me. My stomach drops and my hands get shaky.

So… no, it doesn’t feel like “me.” It feels like my brain throwing that picture at me when I’m trying to do something else.

I’m tense, like I’m waiting for something else to go wrong, and I keep going over how I should’ve stopped what happened.\n\nSorry, I know that’s probably not the neatest answer. I work as a building inspector, so I’m used to looking for what’s unsafe or what might fail, and lately it feels like my brain is doing that constantly, even when I’m not working.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s not even like I’m sitting there trying to think about it. I’ll be doing something normal at work, like measuring a railing, or I’ll hear a ladder scrape somewhere, and suddenly I can see my husband falling like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nAnd then my stomach just drops and my hands get shaky. It’s embarrassing because I’m supposed to be the calm one on a site, you know, checking things, making sure everything’s up to code… but my mind just goes there before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to make the thoughts or images of your husband falling go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way exactly… like I don’t have a specific thing I do every time to make it stop. I mostly just try to get back to whatever I was doing, or I’ll look around the site and remind myself what’s actually in front of me.\n\nIt’s funny, inspectors are always making little checklists—well, not funny, but you know what I mean—and sometimes I catch myself wanting to check everything around me, but I’m not sure if that’s about making the image go away or just… habit from work. So I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, overwhelming urge sounds a little stronger than what I’d say. I do sometimes feel like I need to check things, but that’s also my job, so it gets blurry.\n\nLike on a site, I’m always looking at guardrails and stairs and harness points anyway, and then I’ll remember something from this one renovation where the contractor swore everything was “fine,” and it absolutely wasn’t—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t have a ritual exactly. Not like I have to tap something or count or do a set thing. I mostly just get anxious and try to look around and make sure I know what’s actually there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are something that you want to think about?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t want to think about it. It just kind of barges in. Like I’ll be measuring a railing or hear a ladder scrape, and suddenly I can see him falling like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there choosing to replay it. I might be doing something completely normal, and then my stomach drops and my hands get shaky, and I’m just… there with it. Then I have to try to pull myself back to what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking things around you is a way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, in my job, checking things is literally how you prevent something bad from happening, so it’s hard for me to separate that out.\n\nLike if I’m looking at a railing twice, is that because I’m worried someone’s going to fall, or because the railing actually needs to be checked? I don’t always know. Sometimes I think I’m just doing what I’m supposed to do, and then my mind kind of… goes somewhere else.\n\nSo, maybe a little? But I wouldn’t say I have a clear thought like, “If I don’t check this, something terrible will happen.” It’s more muddled than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something terrible will happen if you don't check things around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that strongly. I don’t usually think, like, “If I don’t check this exact thing, something terrible is going to happen.”\n\nIt’s more that if something seems off, I get this uncomfortable feeling and I want to look again. But then, in construction, that’s also just… normal? You’re supposed to notice things before they become a problem. I had a supervisor once who used to say, “If you walk past it, you own it,” and that kind of sticks in your head.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Sometimes checking calms me down, but I don’t know if I believe it’s preventing some disaster right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are a part of you or something that is being imposed on you?\n\nPatient: It feels more like it’s being pushed on me, I guess. Not like a thought I’m choosing, or something that feels like “me” exactly.\n\nLike I’ll be measuring a railing, or even just hear a ladder scrape nearby, and then out of nowhere I can see my husband falling, like it’s happening right in front of me. And my stomach just drops, my hands get shaky… it’s almost embarrassing because I can be standing there with a tape measure trying to look professional, and suddenly I’m not really thinking about the job anymore.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t feel like a normal part of my thinking. It feels like it cuts in.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to do something in response to these thoughts of your husband falling to make yourself feel better?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll look around or kind of re-check what’s near me, but I don’t know if that’s me responding to the thought or just doing the job.\n\nLike the other day I was on a site and there was this temporary stair setup, and I started thinking about how my husband always skips steps when he’s in a hurry—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess I don’t have some set thing I do to make the thought go away.\n\nI might pause, take a breath, maybe look at whatever triggered it again. But I don’t know if I’d call it “having to” do something. It’s more like I get rattled and then try to get back to what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are something that you would normally think about, or are they completely foreign to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… falling is something I think about sometimes because of my work. Railings, ladders, stairs — that’s normal on a site. You kind of have to think about it, or somebody gets hurt. I’ve seen inspectors miss stuff and then everyone acts like it was obvious after the fact, which is… sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe thoughts about my husband, though, don’t feel like my normal “check the railing” kind of thoughts. I’ll be measuring something, or even just hear a ladder scrape, and all of a sudden I can see him falling, like it’s happening right in front of me. It feels like it comes out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are forcing their way into your mind without you wanting them to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a good way to put it. And then my stomach drops and my hands get shaky.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’m supposed to be focused on the inspection, and instead my brain just kind of yanks me over to that image without asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are unwanted and are interrupting your other thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes, unwanted is exactly it. I don’t mean to think about it.\n\nI’ll be doing something normal, like measuring a railing, or I’ll just hear a ladder scrape nearby — which happens all the time on sites, people are always dragging stuff around — and then out of nowhere I can see my husband falling. Like it’s happening right in front of me. And then my stomach just drops and my hands get shaky.\n\nSo yeah, it interrupts whatever I was thinking about. I’ll be trying to focus on the measurement or what I’m supposed to write down, and suddenly I’m not really there for a second.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the thoughts about your husband falling are consistent with your own beliefs and values?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, I don’t want him hurt. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. It feels like it comes in against my will.

I’ll be trying to do something normal, like measuring a railing, and then—sorry, this is dumb, but the tape measure I use has been sticking lately and that already gets me irritated—and then if I hear a ladder scrape somewhere, it just hits me. I can see my husband falling like it’s happening right in front of me.

I’m not trying to picture it. I don’t sit there and think about it on purpose. It’s just there, and my stomach drops and my hands get shaky before I can even stop it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I can’t seem to shake this heavy feeling. It’s like fear and guilt kind of sitting on me all day. I’m tense, like I’m waiting for something else to go wrong, and I keep going over how I should’ve stopped what happened.\n\nSorry, I know that’s probably not the neatest answer. I work as a building inspector, so I’m used to looking for what’s unsafe or what might fail, and lately it feels like my brain is doing that constantly, even when I’m not working.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s not even like I’m sitting there trying to think about it. I’ll be doing something normal at work, like measuring a railing, or I’ll hear a ladder scrape somewhere, and suddenly I can see my husband falling like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nAnd then my stomach just drops and my hands get shaky. It’s embarrassing because I’m supposed to be the calm one on a site, you know, checking things, making sure everything’s up to code… but my mind just goes there before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to make the thoughts or images of your husband falling go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way exactly… like I don’t have a specific thing I do every time to make it stop. I mostly just try to get back to whatever I was doing, or I’ll look around the site and remind myself what’s actually in front of me.\n\nIt’s funny, inspectors are always making little checklists—well, not funny, but you know what I mean—and sometimes I catch myself wanting to check everything around me, but I’m not sure if that’s about making the image go away or just… habit from work. So I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, overwhelming urge sounds a little stronger than what I’d say. I do sometimes feel like I need to check things, but that’s also my job, so it gets blurry.\n\nLike on a site, I’m always looking at guardrails and stairs and harness points anyway, and then I’ll remember something from this one renovation where the contractor swore everything was “fine,” and it absolutely wasn’t—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t have a ritual exactly. Not like I have to tap something or count or do a set thing. I mostly just get anxious and try to look around and make sure I know what’s actually there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are something that you want to think about?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t want to think about it. It just kind of barges in. Like I’ll be measuring a railing or hear a ladder scrape, and suddenly I can see him falling like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there choosing to replay it. I might be doing something completely normal, and then my stomach drops and my hands get shaky, and I’m just… there with it. Then I have to try to pull myself back to what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking things around you is a way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, in my job, checking things is literally how you prevent something bad from happening, so it’s hard for me to separate that out.\n\nLike if I’m looking at a railing twice, is that because I’m worried someone’s going to fall, or because the railing actually needs to be checked? I don’t always know. Sometimes I think I’m just doing what I’m supposed to do, and then my mind kind of… goes somewhere else.\n\nSo, maybe a little? But I wouldn’t say I have a clear thought like, “If I don’t check this, something terrible will happen.” It’s more muddled than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something terrible will happen if you don't check things around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that strongly. I don’t usually think, like, “If I don’t check this exact thing, something terrible is going to happen.”\n\nIt’s more that if something seems off, I get this uncomfortable feeling and I want to look again. But then, in construction, that’s also just… normal? You’re supposed to notice things before they become a problem. I had a supervisor once who used to say, “If you walk past it, you own it,” and that kind of sticks in your head.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Sometimes checking calms me down, but I don’t know if I believe it’s preventing some disaster right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are a part of you or something that is being imposed on you?\n\nPatient: It feels more like it’s being pushed on me, I guess. Not like a thought I’m choosing, or something that feels like “me” exactly.\n\nLike I’ll be measuring a railing, or even just hear a ladder scrape nearby, and then out of nowhere I can see my husband falling, like it’s happening right in front of me. And my stomach just drops, my hands get shaky… it’s almost embarrassing because I can be standing there with a tape measure trying to look professional, and suddenly I’m not really thinking about the job anymore.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t feel like a normal part of my thinking. It feels like it cuts in.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to do something in response to these thoughts of your husband falling to make yourself feel better?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll look around or kind of re-check what’s near me, but I don’t know if that’s me responding to the thought or just doing the job.\n\nLike the other day I was on a site and there was this temporary stair setup, and I started thinking about how my husband always skips steps when he’s in a hurry—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess I don’t have some set thing I do to make the thought go away.\n\nI might pause, take a breath, maybe look at whatever triggered it again. But I don’t know if I’d call it “having to” do something. It’s more like I get rattled and then try to get back to what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are something that you would normally think about, or are they completely foreign to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… falling is something I think about sometimes because of my work. Railings, ladders, stairs — that’s normal on a site. You kind of have to think about it, or somebody gets hurt. I’ve seen inspectors miss stuff and then everyone acts like it was obvious after the fact, which is… sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe thoughts about my husband, though, don’t feel like my normal “check the railing” kind of thoughts. I’ll be measuring something, or even just hear a ladder scrape, and all of a sudden I can see him falling, like it’s happening right in front of me. My stomach drops and my hands get shaky.\n\nSo it’s not completely foreign in the sense that falling is part of my world, but the way it hits me — that feels foreign. It feels like it comes out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are forcing their way into your mind without you wanting them to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a good way to put it. It’s not like I sit there deciding to imagine it.\n\nI’ll just be doing something normal, like measuring a railing, or I’ll hear a ladder scrape somewhere nearby, and then it’s just there — like I can see my husband falling right in front of me. I’m tense, like I’m waiting for something else to go wrong, and I keep going over how I should’ve stopped what happened.\n\nSorry, I know that’s probably not the neatest answer. I work as a building inspector, so I’m used to looking for what’s unsafe or what might fail, and lately it feels like my brain is doing that constantly, even when I’m not working.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s not even like I’m sitting there trying to think about it. I’ll be doing something normal at work, like measuring a railing, or I’ll hear a ladder scrape somewhere, and suddenly I can see my husband falling like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nAnd then my stomach just drops and my hands get shaky. It’s embarrassing because I’m supposed to be the calm one on a site, you know, checking things, making sure everything’s up to code… but my mind just goes there before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to make the thoughts or images of your husband falling go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way exactly… like I don’t have a specific thing I do every time to make it stop. I mostly just try to get back to whatever I was doing, or I’ll look around the site and remind myself what’s actually in front of me.\n\nIt’s funny, inspectors are always making little checklists—well, not funny, but you know what I mean—and sometimes I catch myself wanting to check everything around me, but I’m not sure if that’s about making the image go away or just… habit from work. So I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, overwhelming urge sounds a little stronger than what I’d say. I do sometimes feel like I need to check things, but that’s also my job, so it gets blurry.\n\nLike on a site, I’m always looking at guardrails and stairs and harness points anyway, and then I’ll remember something from this one renovation where the contractor swore everything was “fine,” and it absolutely wasn’t—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t have a ritual exactly. Not like I have to tap something or count or do a set thing. I mostly just get anxious and try to look around and make sure I know what’s actually there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are something that you want to think about?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t want to think about it. It just kind of barges in. Like I’ll be measuring a railing or hear a ladder scrape, and suddenly I can see him falling like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there choosing to replay it. I might be doing something completely normal, and then my stomach drops and my hands get shaky, and I’m just… there with it. Then I have to try to pull myself back to what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking things around you is a way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, in my job, checking things is literally how you prevent something bad from happening, so it’s hard for me to separate that out.\n\nLike if I’m looking at a railing twice, is that because I’m worried someone’s going to fall, or because the railing actually needs to be checked? I don’t always know. Sometimes I think I’m just doing what I’m supposed to do, and then my mind kind of… goes somewhere else.\n\nSo, maybe a little? But I wouldn’t say I have a clear thought like, “If I don’t check this, something terrible will happen.” It’s more muddled than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something terrible will happen if you don't check things around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that strongly. I don’t usually think, like, “If I don’t check this exact thing, something terrible is going to happen.”\n\nIt’s more that if something seems off, I get this uncomfortable feeling and I want to look again. But then, in construction, that’s also just… normal? You’re supposed to notice things before they become a problem. I had a supervisor once who used to say, “If you walk past it, you own it,” and that kind of sticks in your head.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Sometimes checking calms me down, but I don’t know if I believe it’s preventing some disaster right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are a part of you or something that is being imposed on you?\n\nPatient: It feels more like it’s being pushed on me, I guess. Not like a thought I’m choosing, or something that feels like “me” exactly.\n\nLike I’ll be measuring a railing, or even just hear a ladder scrape nearby, and then out of nowhere I can see my husband falling, like it’s happening right in front of me. And my stomach just drops, my hands get shaky… it’s almost embarrassing because I can be standing there with a tape measure trying to look professional, and suddenly I’m not really thinking about the job anymore.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t feel like a normal part of my thinking. It feels like it cuts in.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to do something in response to these thoughts of your husband falling to make yourself feel better?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll look around or kind of re-check what’s near me, but I don’t know if that’s me responding to the thought or just doing the job.\n\nLike the other day I was on a site and there was this temporary stair setup, and I started thinking about how my husband always skips steps when he’s in a hurry—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess I don’t have some set thing I do to make the thought go away.\n\nI might pause, take a breath, maybe look at whatever triggered it again. But I don’t know if I’d call it “having to” do something. It’s more like I get rattled and then try to get back to what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are something that you would normally think about, or are they completely foreign to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… falling is something I think about sometimes because of my work. Railings, ladders, stairs — that’s normal on a site. You kind of have to think about it, or somebody gets hurt. I’ve seen inspectors miss stuff and then everyone acts like it was obvious after the fact, which is… sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe thoughts about my husband, though, don’t feel like my normal “check the railing” kind of thoughts. I’ll be measuring something, or even just hear a ladder scrape, and all of a sudden I can see him falling, like it’s happening right in front of me. My stomach drops and my hands get shaky.\n\nSo it’s not completely foreign in the sense that falling is part of my world, but the way it hits me — that feels foreign. It feels like it comes out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are forcing their way into your mind without you wanting them to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a good way to put it. It’s not like I sit there deciding to imagine it.\n\nI’ll just be doing something normal, like measuring a railing, or I’ll hear a ladder scrape somewhere nearby, and then it’s just there — like I can see my husband falling right in front of me. And then my stomach drops and my hands get shaky.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’m supposed to be focused on the inspection, and instead my brain just kind of yanks me over to that image without asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are unwanted and are interrupting your other thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes, unwanted is exactly it. I don’t mean to think about it.\n\nI’ll be doing something normal, like measuring a railing, or I’ll just hear a ladder scrape nearby — which happens all the time on sites, people are always dragging stuff around — and then out of nowhere I can see my husband falling. Like it’s happening right in front of me. And then my stomach just drops and my hands get shaky.\n\nSo yeah, it interrupts whatever I was thinking about. I had a supervisor once who used to say, “If you walk past it, you own it,” and that kind of sticks in your head.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Sometimes checking calms me down, but I don’t know if I believe it’s preventing some disaster right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are a part of you or something that is being imposed on you?\n\nPatient: It feels more like it’s being pushed on me, I guess. Not like a thought I’m choosing, or something that feels like “me” exactly.\n\nLike I’ll be measuring a railing, or even just hear a ladder scrape nearby, and then out of nowhere I can see my husband falling, like it’s happening right in front of me. And my stomach just drops, my hands get shaky… it’s almost embarrassing because I can be standing there with a tape measure trying to look professional, and suddenly I’m not really thinking about the job anymore.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t feel like a normal part of my thinking. It feels like it cuts in.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to do something in response to these thoughts of your husband falling to make yourself feel better?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll look around or kind of re-check what’s near me, but I don’t know if that’s me responding to the thought or just doing the job.\n\nLike the other day I was on a site and there was this temporary stair setup, and I started thinking about how my husband always skips steps when he’s in a hurry—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess I don’t have some set thing I do to make the thought go away.\n\nI might pause, take a breath, maybe look at whatever triggered it again. But I don’t know if I’d call it “having to” do something. It’s more like I get rattled and then try to get back to what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are something that you would normally think about, or are they completely foreign to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… falling is something I think about sometimes because of my work. Railings, ladders, stairs — that’s normal on a site. You kind of have to think about it, or somebody gets hurt. I’ve seen inspectors miss stuff and then everyone acts like it was obvious after the fact, which is… sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe thoughts about my husband, though, don’t feel like my normal “check the railing” kind of thoughts. I’ll be measuring something, or even just hear a ladder scrape, and all of a sudden I can see him falling, like it’s happening right in front of me. My stomach drops and my hands get shaky.\n\nSo it’s not completely foreign in the sense that falling is part of my world, but the way it hits me — that feels foreign. It feels like it comes out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are forcing their way into your mind without you wanting them to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a good way to put it. It’s not like I sit there deciding to imagine it.\n\nI’ll just be doing something normal, like measuring a railing, or I’ll hear a ladder scrape somewhere nearby, and then it’s just there — like I can see my husband falling right in front of me. And then my stomach drops and my hands get shaky.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’m supposed to be focused on the inspection, and instead my brain just kind of yanks me over to that image without asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are unwanted and are interrupting your other thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes, unwanted is exactly it. I don’t mean to think about it.\n\nI’ll be doing something normal, like measuring a railing, or I’ll just hear a ladder scrape nearby — which happens all the time on sites, people are always dragging stuff around — and then out of nowhere I can see my husband falling. Like it’s happening right in front of me. And then my stomach just drops and my hands get shaky.\n\nSo yeah, it interrupts whatever I was thinking about. I’ll be trying to focus on the measurement or what I’m supposed to write down, and suddenly I’m not really there for a second.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the thoughts about your husband falling are consistent with your own beliefs and values?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, I don’t want him hurt. That’s the opposite of what I believe or want, obviously. Sorry, that sounds too obvious.\n\nIt’s more like—I can be doing my job, measuring a railing, or I hear a ladder scrape, and then suddenly I see him falling like it’s happening right there. My stomach drops, my hands get shaky, and I’m not choosing it.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t feel like it matches me or what I value. It just barges in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the thoughts about your husband falling are a part of who you are?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s part of who I am. It feels more like something that jumps in front of me.\n\nLike I can be measuring a railing—actually, I was doing that last week and the tape kept slipping, which was annoying—and then I hear a ladder scrape somewhere, and suddenly I can see my husband falling like it’s happening right in front of me. My stomach drops and my hands get shaky.\n\nSo… no, it doesn’t feel like “me.” It feels like my brain throwing that picture at me when I’m trying to do something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are coming into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes. It feels like it comes in against my will.\n\nI’ll be trying to do something normal, like measuring a railing, and then—sorry, this is dumb, but the tape measure I use has been sticking lately and that already gets me irritated—and then if I hear a ladder scrape somewhere, it just hits me. I can see my husband falling like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nI’m not trying to picture it. I don’t sit there and think about it on purpose. It’s just there, and my stomach drops and my hands get shaky before I can even stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must repeat certain actions to reduce your anxiety about your husband falling?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I have, like, a set thing I repeat on purpose.\n\nI mean, I’ll sometimes re-check a railing measurement if I’m already standing there, but that’s kind of my job too, so it’s hard to separate. Actually, the tape measure at work has been driving me nuts because it sticks and then snaps back, and everyone acts like that’s normal, but it makes inspections take longer.\n\nSorry—what was the question again? It’s not even like I’m sitting there trying to think about it. I’ll be doing something normal at work, like measuring a railing, or I’ll hear a ladder scrape somewhere, and suddenly I can see my husband falling like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nAnd then my stomach just drops and my hands get shaky. It’s embarrassing because I’m supposed to be the calm one on a site, you know, checking things, making sure everything’s up to code… but my mind just goes there before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to make the thoughts or images of your husband falling go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way exactly… like I don’t have a specific thing I do every time to make it stop. I mostly just try to get back to whatever I was doing, or I’ll look around the site and remind myself what’s actually in front of me.\n\nIt’s funny, inspectors are always making little checklists—well, not funny, but you know what I mean—and sometimes I catch myself wanting to check everything around me, but I’m not sure if that’s about making the image go away or just… habit from work. So I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific rituals or behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, overwhelming urge sounds a little stronger than what I’d say. I do sometimes feel like I need to check things, but that’s also my job, so it gets blurry.\n\nLike on a site, I’m always looking at guardrails and stairs and harness points anyway, and then I’ll remember something from this one renovation where the contractor swore everything was “fine,” and it absolutely wasn’t—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t have a ritual exactly. Not like I have to tap something or count or do a set thing. I mostly just get anxious and try to look around and make sure I know what’s actually there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are something that you want to think about?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t want to think about it. It just kind of barges in. Like I’ll be measuring a railing or hear a ladder scrape, and suddenly I can see him falling like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there choosing to replay it. I might be doing something completely normal, and then my stomach drops and my hands get shaky, and I’m just… there with it. Then I have to try to pull myself back to what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking things around you is a way to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, in my job, checking things is literally how you prevent something bad from happening, so it’s hard for me to separate that out.\n\nLike if I’m looking at a railing twice, is that because I’m worried someone’s going to fall, or because the railing actually needs to be checked? I don’t always know. Sometimes I think I’m just doing what I’m supposed to do, and then my mind kind of… goes somewhere else.\n\nSo, maybe a little? But I wouldn’t say I have a clear thought like, “If I don’t check this, something terrible will happen.” It’s more muddled than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like something terrible will happen if you don't check things around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that strongly. I don’t usually think, like, “If I don’t check this exact thing, something terrible is going to happen.”\n\nIt’s more that if something seems off, I get this uncomfortable feeling and I want to look again. But then, in construction, that’s also just… normal? You’re supposed to notice things before they become a problem. I had a supervisor once who used to say, “If you walk past it, you own it,” and that kind of sticks in your head.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Sometimes checking calms me down, but I don’t know if I believe it’s preventing some disaster right then.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are a part of you or something that is being imposed on you?\n\nPatient: It feels more like it’s being pushed on me, I guess. Not like a thought I’m choosing, or something that feels like “me” exactly.\n\nLike I’ll be measuring a railing, or even just hear a ladder scrape nearby, and then out of nowhere I can see my husband falling, like it’s happening right in front of me. And my stomach just drops, my hands get shaky… it’s almost embarrassing because I can be standing there with a tape measure trying to look professional, and suddenly I’m not really thinking about the job anymore.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t feel like a normal part of my thinking. It feels like it cuts in.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to do something in response to these thoughts of your husband falling to make yourself feel better?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll look around or kind of re-check what’s near me, but I don’t know if that’s me responding to the thought or just doing the job.\n\nLike the other day I was on a site and there was this temporary stair setup, and I started thinking about how my husband always skips steps when he’s in a hurry—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess I don’t have some set thing I do to make the thought go away.\n\nI might pause, take a breath, maybe look at whatever triggered it again. But I don’t know if I’d call it “having to” do something. It’s more like I get rattled and then try to get back to what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are something that you would normally think about, or are they completely foreign to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… falling is something I think about sometimes because of my work. Railings, ladders, stairs — that’s normal on a site. You kind of have to think about it, or somebody gets hurt. I’ve seen inspectors miss stuff and then everyone acts like it was obvious after the fact, which is… sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe thoughts about my husband, though, don’t feel like my normal “check the railing” kind of thoughts. I’ll be measuring something, or even just hear a ladder scrape, and all of a sudden I can see him falling, like it’s happening right in front of me. My stomach drops and my hands get shaky.\n\nSo it’s not completely foreign in the sense that falling is part of my world, but the way it hits me — that feels foreign. It feels like it comes out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are forcing their way into your mind without you wanting them to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a good way to put it. It’s not like I sit there deciding to imagine it.\n\nI’ll just be doing something normal, like measuring a railing, or I’ll hear a ladder scrape somewhere nearby, and then it’s just there — like I can see my husband falling right in front of me. And then my stomach drops and my hands get shaky.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’m supposed to be focused on the inspection, and instead my brain just kind of yanks me over to that image without asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are unwanted and are interrupting your other thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes, unwanted is exactly it. I don’t mean to think about it.\n\nI’ll be doing something normal, like measuring a railing, or I’ll just hear a ladder scrape nearby — which happens all the time on sites, people are always dragging stuff around — and then out of nowhere I can see my husband falling. Like it’s happening right in front of me. And then my stomach just drops and my hands get shaky.\n\nSo yeah, it interrupts whatever I was thinking about. I’ll be trying to focus on the measurement or what I’m supposed to write down, and suddenly I’m not really there for a second.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the thoughts about your husband falling are consistent with your own beliefs and values?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, I don’t want him hurt. That’s the opposite of what I believe or want, obviously. Sorry, that sounds too obvious.\n\nIt’s more like—I can be doing my job, measuring a railing, or I hear a ladder scrape, and then suddenly I see him falling like it’s happening right there. My stomach drops, my hands get shaky, and I’m not choosing it.\n\nSo no, it doesn’t feel like it matches me or what I value. It just barges in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the thoughts about your husband falling are a part of who you are?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s part of who I am. It feels more like something that jumps in front of me.\n\nLike I can be measuring a railing—actually, I was doing that last week and the tape kept slipping, which was annoying—and then I hear a ladder scrape somewhere, and suddenly I can see my husband falling like it’s happening right in front of me. My stomach drops and my hands get shaky.\n\nSo… no, it doesn’t feel like “me.” It feels like my brain throwing that picture at me when I’m trying to do something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your husband falling are coming into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes. It feels like it comes in against my will.\n\nI’ll be trying to do something normal, like measuring a railing, and then—sorry, this is dumb, but the tape measure I use has been sticking lately and that already gets me irritated—and then if I hear a ladder scrape somewhere, it just hits me. I can see my husband falling like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nI’m not trying to picture it. I don’t sit there and think about it on purpose. It’s just there, and my stomach drops and my hands get shaky before I can even stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must repeat certain actions to reduce your anxiety about your husband falling?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I have, like, a set thing I repeat on purpose.\n\nI mean, I’ll sometimes re-check a railing measurement if I’m already standing there, but that’s kind of my job too, so it’s hard to separate. Actually, the tape measure at work has been driving me nuts because it sticks and then snaps back, and everyone acts like that’s normal, but it makes inspections take longer.\n\nSorry—what was the question again? If I have to repeat certain actions to calm down? I don’t think I’ve noticed that, no. Not in a specific way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the thoughts about your husband falling are in line with your own thoughts and feelings?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, they feel connected to what I’m afraid of, but they don’t feel like my normal thoughts.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll be measuring a railing, or even just hear a ladder scrape, and out of nowhere I can see my husband falling like it’s happening right in front of me. And then my stomach just drops and my hands get shaky.\n\nSorry, I know I keep coming back to the railing thing—it’s just where it happens a lot because of work. But no, it doesn’t feel like it lines up with what I want to be thinking or feeling. It just kind of takes over for a second.\n\nDoctor: Do you find these thoughts about your husband falling to be distressing?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s really upsetting.\n\nLike I’ll just be doing my job, measuring a railing or something, and then I hear a ladder scrape, and suddenly I can see my husband falling like it’s happening right there. And then my stomach drops and my hands start shaking.\n\nIt’s frustrating too, because sometimes I’m trying to focus on the inspection and then I’m thinking about whether the railing height is right, or the tape measure sticking again, and then this image just cuts in. So… yes. It is distressing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. No, I don’t think I’d call it normal for me. I mean, I worry about him like anyone would, but this feels different.

It’s like I’ll be measuring a railing, or I’ll hear a ladder scrape—actually, that sound at sites always makes me look up now—and suddenly I can see him falling, like it’s happening right in front of me. My stomach drops and my hands get shaky before I even really have time to think.

So… no, it doesn’t feel like my regular thinking. It feels like it just barges in.
